Question 1: question_1

❓ Question

How do I request a single GPU in a SLURM submission script on FASRC Cannon?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/gpgpu-computing-on-the-cluster, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise6/run.sbatch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/kempner-partitions

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
To request a single GPU in a SLURM submission script on FASRC Cannon, add the `--gpus` flag to your `#SBATCH` directives: ```bash #SBATCH -p gpu #SBATCH -n 1 #SBATCH --gpus=1 ``` This will allocate one GPU from the `gpu` partition. You can also request CPUs and GPUs independently; for example, to request 1 CPU and 2 GPUs, you would use: ```bash #SBATCH -p gpu #SBATCH -n 1 #SBATCH --gpus=2 ``` When you submit a GPU job, SLURM automatically assigns GPUs and restricts your job to those devices. Inside your code, you reference them using zero-based indexing (e.g., `gpu:0`, `gpu:1`, etc.) [GPU Computing on the FASRC cluster](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/gpgpu-computing-on-the-cluster/). **Note:** Don't forget to load the CUDA module in your script or interactive session: ```bash module load cuda/<version> ```
✅ Expected Answer
Add #SBATCH --gpus=1 to your submission script and SLURM will give the job access to one GPU.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

You can also request CPUs and GPUs independently; for example, to request 1 CPU and 2 GPUs, you would use: ```bash #SBATCH -p gpu #SBATCH -n 1 #SBATCH --gpus=2 ``` When you submit a GPU job, SLURM automatically assigns GPUs and restricts your job to those devices. Inside your code, you reference them using zero-based indexing (e.g., `gpu:0`, `gpu:1`, etc.) [GPU Computing on the FASRC cluster](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/gpgpu-computing-on-the-cluster/). **Note:** Don't forget to load the CUDA module in your script or interactive session: ```bash module load cuda/<version> ```

Question 3: question_3

❓ Question

How much memory does a SLURM job get on Cannon if I don't specify a memory request?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, slurm.schedmd.com/cons_tres.html

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
If you don't specify a memory request, your SLURM job on Cannon gets a default of **100 MB per node** [Slurm memory limits](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/). This is intentionally small. If your job uses more than that, it will be killed with an `OUT_OF_MEMORY` error [Slurm memory limits](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/). To request more memory, use the `--mem` flag in your `sbatch` or `salloc` command. For example: ```bash sbatch --mem=8G my_script.sh ``` This requests 8 GB of memory per node. If you need memory per CPU core instead, you can use `--mem-per-cpu` [Slurm Workload Manager - srun](https://slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html).
✅ Expected Answer
If you omit the memory request you are granted 100 MB by default, which is usually too little and will likely cause the job to be killed for going over.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Args: How much memory does a SLURM job get on Cannon if I don't specify a memory request?
🤖 Assistant Message #2
If you don't specify a memory request, your SLURM job on Cannon gets a default of **100 MB per node** [Slurm memory limits](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/). This is intentionally small. If your job uses more than that, it will be killed with an `OUT_OF_MEMORY` error [Slurm memory limits](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/). To request more memory, use the `--mem` flag in your `sbatch` or `salloc` command. For example: ```bash sbatch --mem=8G my_script.sh ``` This requests 8 GB of memory per node. If you need memory per CPU core instead, you can use `--mem-per-cpu` [Slurm Workload Manager - srun](https://slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html).

Question 4: question_4

❓ Question

What is the storage quota for a FASRC user home directory?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Your FASRC home directory has a **100 GB hard quota** and a **95 GB soft quota** that triggers notifications when you approach the limit [Home directory full](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full/). You can check your current usage and quota with: ```bash quota $HOME ``` If you're over quota or approaching it, you'll need to remove files to get back under the limit [Home directory full](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full/).
✅ Expected Answer
Each user gets a 100 GB home directory, enforced as a hard limit.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Question 5: question_5

❓ Question

How much storage does a FASRC lab group directory get, and how many files?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-ownership-and-access-policy, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-modernization-initiative-faqs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-deletion-recommendations

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Each FASRC lab group receives a **4 TiB** lab directory (located at `/n/holylabs`) with a **1 million file** limit [Home and Lab directories](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage/). Key details about the lab directory: * **Size Limit:** 4 TiB (hard limit) * **File Limit:** 1 million files * **Availability:** Accessible from all cluster nodes, but cannot be mounted on desktops/laptops * **Backup:** Highly redundant storage, but no backups/snapshots * **Retention:** Data is retained for the duration of the lab group * **Performance:** Moderate — not appropriate for I/O-intensive or large numbers of jobs [Home and Lab directories](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage/) For checking usage, you can run `quota /n/holylabs/<lab_name>` to see the lab group's space and file counts [Checking quota and usage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage/).
✅ Expected Answer
Each lab gets a 4 TiB lab directory as a hard limit, with a 1 million file limit.

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
On the Cannon filesystem, FASRC lab directories reside at **`/n/holylabs`** [Getting Started with FASRC Storage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage/). Each lab is allocated 4TB of storage in this location. Note that this storage cannot be expanded and does not include backups or snapshots [Getting Started with FASRC Storage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage/).
✅ Expected Answer
Lab directories reside under /n/holylabs.

Question 7: question_7

❓ Question

What hostname do I SSH to for command-line access to the FASRC cluster?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
To access the FASRC cluster via SSH, connect to: **`login.rc.fas.harvard.edu`** You can also target specific datacenters if needed: - **Boston:** `boslogin.rc.fas.harvard.edu` - **Holyoke:** `holylogin.rc.fas.harvard.edu` The full SSH command looks like: ```bash ssh USERNAME@login.rc.fas.harvard.edu ``` where `USERNAME` is your FASRC account name (e.g., `jharvard`). Login nodes are shared, multi-user resources intended as gateways — not for running jobs or heavy applications. For production work, submit jobs via Slurm or use interactive sessions on compute nodes. For more details, see [Command line access with Terminal (login nodes)](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access/).
✅ Expected Answer
SSH to login.rc.fas.harvard.edu using your FASRC username, password, and OpenAuth token.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide

What command lists the modules I currently have loaded on Cannon?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/amber.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/parallel-matlab-pct-dcs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/centos7-singularity, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/knime-on-the-fasrc-clusters

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
To see which modules are currently loaded, run: ```bash module list ``` This command displays all the environment modules active in your current shell session [Lmod software modules – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro/). If you want to see what modules are *available* to load (not just what's loaded), you can use `module avail` or `module spider`.
✅ Expected Answer
Run module list to display all modules currently loaded in your shell environment.

❓ Question

What command shows my lab's fairshare information on FASRC?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/dual-lab-affiliations-on-cluster, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/kempner-partitions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/additional-groups, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/common-pitfalls, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/R/Large_Data_Processing_R/parallel_computation/R_parLapply_vdi.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/seas-compute-resources, slurm.schedmd.com/quickstart.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/lab-groups, slurm.schedmd.com/qos.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
I found it! According to the FASRC FAQ: To check your lab's fairshare score, run: ```bash sshare -u RCUSERNAME ``` Replace `RCUSERNAME` with your FASRC username. The effective score appears in the last column of the output. As a rule of thumb: - **≤ 0.5** = lower priority - **≥ 0.5** = higher priority See the full explanation at [Frequently Asked Questions (FAQ)](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq) and the [Fairshare and Job Accounting](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/) guide.
✅ Expected Answer
Use sshare (e.g. sshare --account=<lab> -a) to see RawShares, NormShares, RawUsage, EffectvUsage, and the FairShare score for an account.

  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Question 10: question_10

❓ Question

How long are FASRC home directory snapshots kept?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fas-rc-research-data-retention-and-deletion-policy, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
FASRC home directory snapshots are retained for **2 weeks** [Home and Lab directories – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage/). The snapshots are stored in a hidden `.snapshot` directory in your home directory and include hourly, daily, and weekly snapshots. To restore older files, you can copy them from the appropriate `.snapshot` subdirectory. **Important notes:** - If you delete your entire home directory, the snapshots are also deleted and are **not recoverable** [Home and Lab directories – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage/) - Home directories have a 100 GB hard limit and are not suitable for I/O-intensive workloads or storing sensitive data (HRCI/level 3 or above) [Home and Lab directories – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage/)
✅ Expected Answer
Home directories have daily snapshots retained for 2 weeks, accessible via the .snapshot directory.

Question 11: question_11

❓ Question

My SLURM job on Cannon was killed for running out of memory. How do I find out how much memory it actually used, and how do I request more next time?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Here is how to check what your job actually used and how to request more memory next time. ### 1. Find out how much memory your job used * **For a completed (successful) job:** Use `sacct` with the `--format` option to see the peak memory used. ```bash sacct -j YOUR_JOB_ID --format=JobID,MaxRSS,AllocCPUS,State ``` * `MaxRSS` shows the maximum resident set size (memory) used by the job. * **For a running job:** Use the `jobstats` command. ```bash jobstats YOUR_JOB_ID ``` This will show you the current memory usage of the running job. * **For a job that failed with `OUT_OF_MEMORY`:** Be aware that **Slurm's recorded memory usage will be inaccurate** for these jobs because the process was killed before it could report its final usage. To get an accurate measurement, you must run the job successfully (with enough memory) and then check the usage. ### 2. Request more memory for your next job Add the `--mem` directive to your `#SBATCH` script. You can specify the amount in MB or GB. * **Request 4 GB of memory:** ```bash #SBATCH --mem 4G ``` * **Request 8000 MB of memory:** ```bash #SBATCH --mem 8000 ``` **Tips for choosing the right amount:** * Start by requesting a bit more than what `jobstats` or `sacct` reported for a successful run. * Job slots on Cannon average about 4000 MB per core, but most jobs use less. Requesting too much memory can make it harder for the scheduler to find a node for your job. * For multi-node jobs, `--mem` sets the limit **per node**. Make sure you are distributing tasks evenly (e.g., using `--ntasks-per-node`) so memory usage is consistent across nodes.
✅ Expected Answer
After the job ends, run sacct -j <jobid> --format=JobID,Elapsed,ReqMem,MaxRSS to compare the peak memory used (MaxRSS) against what was requested (ReqMem). In your next submit script raise the request with --mem=<N>G (whole job) or --mem-per-cpu=<N>G, setting it a bit above the observed MaxRSS so normal variation doesn't kill the job.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Question 12: question_12

❓ Question

Is the two-factor code for logging into FASRC the same as my Harvard Key code? If not, how do I set it up?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/duo-mobile, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
**No, the two-factor code for logging into FASRC is not the same as your Harvard Key code.** They are separate tokens, but you can use the same Duo Mobile app to generate both. ### How to set up your RC two-factor code in Duo Mobile 1. **On a computer or tablet (not your phone):** - Go to [https://two-factor.rc.fas.harvard.edu](https://two-factor.rc.fas.harvard.edu/) and log in with your RC username and password. - You'll see a page explaining that a link was sent to your email. - Open that email and click the link (or paste it into your browser). - This will display a **QR code** for your RC OpenAuth token. Leave this page open. 2. **On your mobile device (with a camera):** - Open the **Duo Mobile** app. - Tap the **+** icon to add a new token. - Use your camera to scan the QR code displayed on your computer screen. - Confirm the token name/icon when prompted. Once added, you'll see two entries in Duo Mobile: one for Harvard Key and one for RC. Tap the key icon next to your RC token to view the 6-digit code, which you can use to log into the cluster, connect to the RC VPN, and more [Using Duo Mobile to display your RC two-factor code](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/duo-mobile/).
✅ Expected Answer
No. FASRC has its own two-factor system (OpenAuth) that is separate from Harvard Key. Set it up with a smartphone authenticator app (e.g. Google Authenticator or Duo) before accessing cluster resources; you use this OpenAuth token along with your FASRC username and password.

Question 13: question_13

❓ Question

How do I find which versions of a software package are available as modules on Cannon, and how should I load it so my workflow keeps working when defaults change?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/knime-on-the-fasrc-clusters, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/r-parallel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
To find available versions and load a module reliably on Cannon: ### 1. Find available versions - **`module avail <name>`** — shows versions you can load in your current environment. For example: ``` module avail matlab ``` This lists only the versions compatible with your currently loaded compiler/MPI stack [Lmod software modules](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro/). - **`module spider <name>`** — shows *all* versions of a package, including those that require different compilers or MPI. This is the most robust way to see the full inventory [Lmod software modules](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro/). - **`module --default avail`** — shows the full list regardless of your current loaded modules, which is useful when `module avail` is too narrow [Lmod software modules](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro/). ### 2. Load a specific version (recommended) Always specify the full module name with version to protect your workflow from default changes: ```bash module load matlab/R2025b-fasrc01 module load gcc/15.2.0-fasrc01 openmpi/5.0.8-fasrc01 hdf5/1.14.6-fasrc01 ``` This ensures reproducibility even if the default version changes [Lmod software modules](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro/). ### 3. Avoid relying on defaults You *can* load a module without a version (e.g., `module load matlab`), which loads the latest default version, but this is **not recommended** for workflows because the default may change over time [Lmod software modules](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro/). ### Quick tips - **`module list`** — shows what's currently loaded. - **`module purge`** — unloads everything, useful for a clean slate. - **`module save <name>`** / **`module restore <name>`** — save and restore a collection of modules for reuse [Lmod software modules](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro/).
✅ Expected Answer
Search with module spider <name> to list all available modules matching the string regardless of your current environment. Then load with both the name and a specific version (module load <name>/<version>) — FASRC strongly recommends this so you are protected if the default version changes later.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro

Question 14: question_14

❓ Question

What's the recommended way to set up an isolated Python environment for a project on Cannon?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python-package-installation, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Python/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tensorflow, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/gReLU/grelu.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/jupyter-notebook-server-on-cluster

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The recommended way to set up an isolated Python environment on Cannon is to use **Mamba** (a faster drop-in replacement for Conda) inside a virtual environment. Direct use of `pip` outside a virtual environment is discouraged on FASRC clusters. Here's the recommended workflow: 1. **Start an interactive session** (required for creating environments): ```bash salloc --partition test --nodes=1 --cpus-per-task=2 --mem=4GB --time=0-02:00:00 ``` 2. **Load the Python module** (which includes Mamba): ```bash module load python ``` 3. **Create an environment** (by default, environments are stored in `~/.conda/envs`): ```bash mamba create -n <ENV_NAME> ``` Or create and install packages simultaneously: ```bash mamba create -n <ENV_NAME> python=3.11 pip wheel ``` 4. **Activate the environment**: ```bash source activate <ENV_NAME> ``` 5. **Install packages** as needed: ```bash mamba install -y <PACKAGE> ``` **Important notes:** - The FASRC `python` module is built with Miniforge3 and uses `conda-forge` as the default channel, since `repo.anaconda.com` is blocked on the cluster due to licensing restrictions [Python Package Installation – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python-package-installation) - If you want to store packages in a different location (e.g., a Lab shared directory), set the environment variables `CONDA_PKGS_DIRS` and `CONDA_ENVS_PATH` before creating the environment [Python Package Installation – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python-package-installation)
✅ Expected Answer
Load a module that provides mamba (python/3*, Miniforge3, or Mambaforge), then create an isolated environment, e.g. mamba create -n data_env python=3.9 pandas numpy, and activate it with source activate data_env. FASRC recommends using Miniforge with mamba so dependencies are isolated and you avoid conflicts rather than working in the base environment.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python

Question 15: question_15

❓ Question

How is my job's priority determined on Cannon, and how can I see the priority of my pending jobs?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html, slurm.schedmd.com/priority_multifactor.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, slurm.schedmd.com/quickstart.html, slurm.schedmd.com/priority_multifactor.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
On Cannon, your job's priority is determined by two main factors: 1. **FairShare Score** – This reflects your lab's account usage relative to its allocated "Share" of the cluster. Your lab receives a base Share (from the FAS Gratis Share) plus any additional Share from purchased hardware. The FairShare score is calculated based on how much of that Share you've actually used. A higher FairShare score (i.e., you haven't used your full allocation) results in higher job priority. The FairShare score is multiplied by a weighting factor of 10,000,000 to produce an integer priority component [Fairshare and Job Accounting](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/). 2. **Job Age** – Jobs that have been waiting longer in the queue gain priority over time, ensuring that long-pending jobs eventually get scheduled [Fairshare and Job Accounting](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/). ### How to see your pending jobs' priority You can view the priority of your pending jobs using the `squeue` command with the `--start` or priority-specific formatting. For example: ```bash squeue -u $USER -o "%.10i %.9P %.20j %.8T %.10M %.6D %.20S %P" ``` The last column (`%P`) will show the job's priority value. You can also use: ```bash squeue -u $USER --start ``` This will show estimated start times, which indirectly reflect priority (higher-priority jobs will have earlier estimated start times). For more details on your account's FairShare score, you can check the FairShare dashboard or run: ```bash fairshare ``` This will show your account's current FairShare score and usage statistics.
✅ Expected Answer
A job's priority combines two parts: your FairShare score multiplied by a weighting factor (10,000,000) to make it an integer, plus job age, which accrues while the job waits and caps at 3 days on Cannon. Inspect the breakdown for pending jobs with sprio, or use showq -o -p <partition> to see the pending queue ordered by priority.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Question 16: question_16

❓ Question

I need to move a very large dataset to the cluster. Should I use Globus, and can I transfer it straight into my home directory?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
**Yes, use Globus** for very large datasets — it's designed for high-throughput transfers and is the recommended alternative to SCP/SSH for large data [Globus File Transfer – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/). **No, you cannot transfer directly to your home directory via Globus.** Home directories are explicitly excluded from Globus access for security reasons (to avoid inadvertently sharing authentication keys or passwords) [Globus File Transfer – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/). ### Where to transfer instead: - **Lab storage** (`/n/holylabs`) — 4TB, no retention limit, no snapshots - **netscratch** (`/n/netscratch`) — 50TB, 90-day retention policy - **Compute Storage** or **Lab Storage** (paid options) Globus can only see folders inside `Lab` or `Users/$USER` within your lab share, so you'll need to transfer your data there first [Globus File Transfer – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/). ### Next steps: 1. Log in to [Globus](https://www.globus.org/) with your HarvardKey 2. Select the appropriate Harvard FAS RC collection (Holyoke or Boston, depending on your data center) 3. Transfer your data to your lab share Would you like help with the Globus setup steps or choosing the right storage location for your dataset?
✅ Expected Answer
Yes, use Globus — it's the recommended alternative to SSH/SCP specifically for very large data sets, and you can install Globus Connect Personal to make your own machine an endpoint. However, you cannot transfer into your home directory: home directories are not available through Globus (and never will be) because sharing them is a security vulnerability. Target a lab or scratch location instead.

Question 17: question_17

❓ Question

I accidentally deleted some files in my home directory on Cannon. Can I get them back, and is there any situation where I can't?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rdiff-backup, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fas-rc-research-data-retention-and-deletion-policy

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes — **you can usually get them back**, because FASRC regularly backs up home directories. The standard recovery method is to use **`rdiff-backup`** to restore files from a previous backup. ### How to recover deleted files 1. **List available backup versions** of your home directory: ```bash rdiff-backup -l /path/to/your/home-backup ``` This shows timestamps of past incremental backups. 2. **Restore a file from the latest backup**: ```bash rdiff-backup --restore-as-of now /path/to/your/home-backup/your/file /place/to/put/it ``` 3. **Restore from a specific time** (e.g., 1 week ago): ```bash rdiff-backup --restore-as-of 1W /path/to/your/home-backup/your/file /place/to/put/it ``` See the full guide at [rdiff-backup – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rdiff-backup/). ### When you *can't* recover files You may **not** be able to recover files if: - **No backup exists for the time period** you need — `rdiff-backup` only restores from available incremental snapshots. If the file was deleted before the most recent backup, and no earlier backup contains it, it won't be recoverable. - **The backup destination itself is corrupted or unavailable** — though FASRC maintains these backups, severe infrastructure issues could temporarily prevent access. - **You've exceeded your home directory quota** and can't write the restored files back — in that case, you may need to free up space first or restore to a different location (e.g., your scratch or lab directory). ### Recommendation Act quickly: the more time that passes, the more likely the file has been overwritten or purged from older incremental backups. If you're unsure which backup version contains the file, use `rdiff-backup -l` to browse available versions, or contact FASRC support for assistance.
✅ Expected Answer
Usually yes: home directories have daily snapshots retained for 2 weeks, so you can recover recently deleted files from the .snapshot directory. The exception is if you delete your entire home directory — that also deletes the snapshots, and it is not recoverable.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

❓ Question

I want to run a short GPU job on Cannon. How do I request the GPU and make sure it has enough memory so it isn't killed?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-defense-shield, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/GPU_Computing/Dist_GPU_Computing/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise3/run.sbatch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/configure.sh, slurm.schedmd.com/resource_limits.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
1) **Summary of Attempt**: The agent attempted to retrieve specific SLURM configuration details for requesting GPUs and memory limits on the Cannon cluster by performing multiple hybrid vector searches. However, the process hit the maximum recursion limit of 50 iterations without finding a definitive, single-step answer, resulting in an interrupted run. 2) **Best Possible Answer**: Based on the retrieved documentation for the FASRC Cannon cluster, here is how to request a GPU and prevent memory-related job termination: * **Requesting the GPU**: Use the `--gres` (Generic Resources) flag in your `sbatch` script or `salloc` command. For a single GPU, use `--gres=gpu:1`. * **Preventing Memory Kill**: To ensure the job isn't killed due to memory limits, explicitly request sufficient memory using the `--mem` flag. The default memory limit might be too low for GPU workloads. For example, use `--mem=32G` (or higher, depending on your model/data size) to reserve enough RAM for the host process managing the GPU. * **Partition Selection**: Ensure you specify the correct partition, such as `--partition=gpu` or `--partition=gpu_test` (if available for testing), as GPU nodes are often in separate partitions. **Example `sbatch` script snippet:** ```bash #!/bin/bash #SBATCH --job-name=MyGPUJob #SBATCH --partition=gpu # Specify GPU partition #SBATCH --gres=gpu:1 # Request 1 GPU #SBATCH --mem=32G # Request 32GB RAM to prevent OOM kills #SBATCH --time=01:00:00 # Time limit #SBATCH --output=job.out # Your command here python my_gpu_script.py ``` 3) **Note**: This run stopped after hitting the recursion limit of 50.
✅ Expected Answer
Request the GPU with #SBATCH --gpus=1. Don't rely on the default memory — if you omit a memory request you only get 100 MB and the job will likely be killed, so set #SBATCH --mem=<N>G to a value your job needs. Submit to a GPU-capable partition and keep within its time limit (for short tests, use a test/short partition).

Question 23: question_23

❓ Question

What does someone with non-faculty PI rights have to supply at signup that an ordinary researcher does not?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/how-do-i-get-a-research-computing-account

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation, someone with **non-faculty PI rights** must supply the following at signup that an ordinary researcher does not: 1. **A supported affiliation** – They must have a PI role with a supported affiliation (e.g., FAS, SEAS, etc.). 2. **Their own 33-digit billing string(s)** – Unlike ordinary researchers (who are sponsored by a PI), non-faculty PIs must provide their own billing information. Additionally, their account and PI rights **will need to be vetted before approval**, and FASRC may contact them for more information. This is in contrast to ordinary researchers (postdocs, grad students, undergrads, staff), who simply need a PI who belongs to a supported affiliation to approve/sponsor their account, without providing their own billing strings. Source: [Account Qualifications and Affiliations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications/)
✅ Expected Answer
A NON-FACULTY PI must supply a 33-digit billing code at signup. Non-Faculty PI accounts also carry additional controls and privileges, and the signup requires vetting so it takes longer to process.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications

Question 24: question_24

❓ Question

Our lab has grant money and wants to buy extra compute nodes for the cluster. Can we just have them racked whenever we're ready?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/jupyter-notebook-server-on-cluster, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No, you cannot simply rack extra compute nodes whenever you're ready. According to FASRC documentation, purchasing and installing hardware for the cluster is a coordinated process that takes time: > "This is not an immediate solution to the problem as it takes time for hardware to be built and installed. That said once the hardware arrives your Share will be increased and your fairshare will improve commensurately. Please [contact](https://www.rc.fas.harvard.edu/about/contact) FASRC for more information if you wish to purchase hardware for the cluster." [Fairshare and Job Accounting](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/) The process involves: 1. **Contacting FASRC** to coordinate the purchase and installation 2. **Hardware procurement and build time** - it's not an immediate process 3. **Integration into the cluster** - FASRC manages the rack installation and cluster integration Once the hardware is installed, your lab's Share (which affects your fairshare priority on the cluster) will be increased based on the hardware specifications. If you're interested in purchasing additional compute capacity, you should contact FASRC directly at [https://www.rc.fas.harvard.edu/about/contact](https://www.rc.fas.harvard.edu/about/contact) to discuss the process and timeline.
✅ Expected Answer
No. FASRC will discuss your computing needs, coordinate a quote, and install the new hardware, but as of the 2019 cluster refresh and the move to water-cooling, new nodes can be added into the cluster only at certain times of the year.

🎯 Retrieval Check

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The agent attempted to answer the question by performing multiple hybrid vector searches on the FAS Research Computing documentation, specifically querying for policies regarding outside collaborator billing and account access upon a PI's departure. However, the process hit the recursion limit of 50 without retrieving specific policy details in the truncated context provided. Based on standard Harvard FAS Research Computing (FASRC) policies which govern these accounts: 1. **Who pays?** The sponsoring PI (or their grant) is responsible for paying for the outside collaborator's compute time. Outside collaborators do not have their own billing accounts; their usage is charged to the sponsor's grant. 2. **What happens if the PI leaves?** The outside collaborator's access is typically terminated or suspended when the sponsoring PI leaves the institution or their grant ends. The collaborator must find a new sponsor within the FASRC community to maintain access, or their account will be deactivated. The run stopped after hitting the recursion limit of 50.
✅ Expected Answer
There is no up-front cost for the collaborator's account, but their cluster usage (in CPU hours) is applied to the sponsoring PI's lab group fairshare and billed to the school on a quarterly basis. The account must be requested as EXTERNAL and be approved by and attached to an existing HCSPH PI's FASRC account. Should the PI leave HCSPH, the collaborator's access also ends unless it is transferred to another PI's account and billing; it is the PI's responsibility to notify FASRC when the collaborator's access should be withdrawn.

Question 28: question_28

❓ Question

I'm on FASSE and want to run VS Code on a compute node. Can I just set up Remote-SSH to the compute node the way I do on Cannon?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/vscode_remote_tunnel.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cursor-remote-development-via-ssh-and-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/knime-on-the-fasrc-clusters, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/about-usernames, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cursor-remote-development-via-ssh-and-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/proxy-settings

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
**Summary of Attempt:** The agent searched for documentation regarding VS Code Remote SSH and Tunnel setups on FASRC clusters (specifically Cannon and FASSE). It retrieved specific configuration details for using `ProxyCommand` to tunnel SSH connections through the Cannon login node to access compute nodes, including examples for both GPU and non-GPU partitions. **Answer:** No, you cannot simply set up Remote-SSH to the compute node directly in the same way you might on Cannon if you are accustomed to direct login. On FASSE (and Cannon), compute nodes are not directly accessible via standard SSH from the outside; you must use a **ProxyCommand** to tunnel through the login node (`cannon` or `fasselogin`). To run VS Code on a FASSE compute node, you should use the **ProxyCommand approach** documented in the FASRC guides: 1. **Configure `~/.ssh/config`**: Set up a ProxyCommand that uses `ssh -q cannon` (or the appropriate FASSE login node) to execute a `salloc` command. This command requests an interactive job on a compute node and sets up a netcat (`nc`) listener on port 22. 2. **Example ProxyCommand**: ```ssh-config Host compute-node ProxyCommand ssh -q cannon "salloc --immediate=180 --job-name=vscode --partition <partition> --gres=gpu:1 --time=0-01:00 --mem=4GB --quiet /bin/bash -c 'echo $SLURM_JOBID > ~/vscode-job-id; nc $SLURM_NODELIST 22'" User <your_username> HostName localhost Port 22 ``` 3. **Connect**: Use VS Code's Remote-SSH extension to connect to `compute-node`. This will automatically allocate a job on a compute node and tunnel your VS Code session to it. Alternatively, you can use the **Remote Tunnel** approach, which involves installing the VS Code CLI on the cluster and establishing a tunnel, but the ProxyCommand method is generally preferred for direct SSH-like access to specific compute nodes. **Note:** The run stopped after hitting the recursion limit of 50.
✅ Expected Answer
No. You cannot use Remote SSH to connect your local VS Code to a FASSE compute node, because salloc is not permitted on FASSE for security reasons. Remote SSH to FASSE's login node does work. For a FASSE compute node, launching a Remote Tunnel as a batch job (Approach I) is your only option — and before doing that you must enable internet access by setting a network proxy (you are on the FASSE VPN realm, @fasse), including adding the same proxy in your local VS Code settings; remember to unset that proxy in VS Code when you are not on the FASSE VPN, or it will block your other non-FASSE VS Code remote sessions.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

Question 29: question_29

❓ Question

The VS Code Dev Containers extension keeps trying to talk to a Docker daemon on the cluster, which doesn't exist there. What do I have to change so it uses the container runtime FASRC actually provides?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/containers, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/podman, slurm.schedmd.com/containers.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/podman, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/podman

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
FASRC provides **Podman** (not Docker) on the clusters, and the Dev Containers extension expects a Docker daemon by default. To make it work, you need to: ### 1. Start the Podman system service on the compute node In a VS Code terminal on the cluster, run: ```bash nohup podman system service -t 0 >/dev/null 2>&1 & ``` This starts a Podman API socket at `/tmp/containers-user-$(id -u)/podman/podman.sock` on the compute node. ### 2. Update your `devcontainer.json` Add the following settings so Dev Containers knows to use the Podman socket instead of looking for a Docker daemon: ```json { "remoteUser": "root", "mounts": [ "source=/tmp/podman-run-<UID>/podman/podman.sock,target=/var/run/docker-host.sock,type=bind" ], "remoteEnv": { "DOCKER_BUILDKIT": "0" } } ``` Replace `<UID>` with your actual user ID (e.g., `1234`). ### Why this works - FASRC clusters use **Podman**, which is daemonless and doesn't have a persistent Docker-like daemon running on the node [Podman – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/podman/). - The `podman system service` command starts a temporary API socket that Dev Containers can connect to. - The `mounts` entry binds that socket into the container as `/var/run/docker-host.sock`, and Dev Containers uses it as a Docker-compatible backend. > **Note:** You'll need to restart the `podman system service` each time you start a new compute node session, since the socket is per-node and per-session.
✅ Expected Answer
Point the Dev Containers extension at Podman. In Code > Settings > Extensions > Dev Containers, change 'Dev > Containers: Docker Path' from 'docker' to 'podman', and change 'Dev > Containers: Docker Socket Path' from '/var/run/docker.sock' to '/tmp/podman-run-<uid>/podman/podman.sock', replacing <uid> with your FASRC user ID (obtained by running 'id -u' on the cluster). If you are using a local VS Code rather than an OOD Remote Desktop, you will need to revert these settings later if you use dev containers with a local Docker installation.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel
💬 Agent Messages (4)

🛠️ Tool Call #2: fetch_catalog_document
Args: No query found.
🛠️ Tool Call #3: search_vectorstore_hybrid

Question 30: question_30

❓ Question

I set up TF 2.16 on the cluster with the FASRC conda steps and now Keras blows up saying it has no backend. What environment variable am I supposed to export?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tensorflow

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tensorflow, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rstudio-server-vs-rstudio-desktop, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/r-and-rstudio, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/alphapose.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
You need to export the `KERAS_BACKEND` environment variable: ```bash export KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow" ``` This is required starting with TensorFlow 2.16.1, which bundles Keras 3.0. Without it, Keras doesn't know which backend to use and will fail. You can add this to your conda environment's activation script so it's set automatically whenever you activate the environment: ```bash mkdir -p $CONDA_PREFIX/etc/conda/activate.d echo 'export KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow"' >> $CONDA_PREFIX/etc/conda/activate.d/env_vars.sh ``` See the FASRC TensorFlow documentation for full setup details [TensorFlow – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tensorflow/).
✅ Expected Answer
Export KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow". Starting with version 2.16.1, TensorFlow includes Keras 3.0, so the Keras backend must be set to TF. FASRC's instructions include `export KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow"` in both the GPU and CPU conda recipes, and prefix the singularity runs with it as well, e.g. `KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow" singularity exec --nv tf2.16.1_gpu.simg python3`.

Question 32: question_32

❓ Question

My PyTorch job on gpu_test sees a device but the card looks sliced up and I only get a fraction of it. What do I need to do differently on that partition?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/mmpose.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Example_Recipes/pytorch/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
On the `gpu_test` partition, the GPUs are configured in **MIG (Multi-Instance GPU) mode**, which slices the physical GPU into smaller instances. PyTorch needs to know which MIG instance to use. To fix this, you need to set the `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES` environment variable to the MIG instance name. Here's how: 1. **Find the MIG instance name:** ```bash nvidia-smi -L ``` 2. **Set the environment variable:** ```bash export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=MIG-5b36b802-0ab0-5f37-af2d-ac23f40ef62d ``` *(Replace with the actual MIG identifier from your `nvidia-smi -L` output.)* 3. **Or automate it:** ```bash export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=$(nvidia-smi -L | awk '/MIG/ {gsub(/[()]/,"");print $NF}') ``` This step is **only required on the `gpu_test` partition** — it's not necessary on the regular `gpu` partition. See the FASRC PyTorch documentation for more details [PyTorch](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch/).
✅ Expected Answer
gpu_test is currently the only FASRC partition with MIG (multi-instance GPU) mode enabled, so you must target a MIG instance explicitly. Get the GPU/MIG instance name with `nvidia-smi -L`, then set CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES to the MIG instance, e.g. `export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=MIG-5b36b802-0ab0-5f37-af2d-ac23f40ef62d`, or automate it with `export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=$(nvidia-smi -L | awk '/MIG/ {gsub(/[()]/,"");print $NF}')`.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch

🤖 Assistant Message #2

Question 33: question_33

❓ Question

Our CryoSPARC master keeps coming up bound to the wrong machine name so the workers can't reach it. Where is that hostname actually set?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cryosparc

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cryosparc, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/bashrc_additions.sh, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/configure.sh, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The hostname is set in the CryoSPARC Master configuration file: **`cryosparc_master/config.sh`** Look for the `CRYOSPARC_MASTER_HOSTNAME` variable: ```bash export CRYOSPARC_MASTER_HOSTNAME=holy_______.rc.fas.harvard.edu ``` You'll need to: 1. Edit `$INSTALL_DIR/cryosparc_master/config.sh` and set `CRYOSPARC_MASTER_HOSTNAME` to the correct hostname of the node where the master is running. 2. Restart the master so it picks up the change: ```shell cryosparcm restart ``` If you also changed the port, you'll need to run `cryosparcm fixdbport` before restarting. See the FASRC CryoSPARC setup guide for full details: [CryoSPARC – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cryosparc/) and the [FASRC User_Codes README](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/README.md).
✅ Expected Answer
It is set in the CryoSPARC master config file, `cryosparc_master/config.sh`, which holds the license, the path to the MongoDB database, the master hostname, and the base TCP port. The hostname of the node running the master application is typically hardcoded in that config.sh; if it is left unset, CryoSPARC takes the hostname of the machine on which `cryosparcm start` is called. If these config.sh settings are not correct you will experience errors. Note also that the TCP ports of the individual component programs are not individually configurable — only the base port the user connects to is.

Question 35: question_35

❓ Question

Google finally approved my AlphaFold 3 request and sent me the weights as a .bin.zst download. Where am I supposed to unpack that on Cannon — can I just leave it in netscratch?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/alphafold_v3.def, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/how-do-i-get-a-research-computing-account, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
**No — do not leave it in `netscratch`.** Per the FASRC AlphaFold documentation, you must place the extracted model weights in a **lab share** (not `netscratch`), because that path will be used as your `--model_dir` when running AlphaFold 3 [AlphaFold – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold/). Here's what to do: 1. **Unpack the file** on Cannon: ```bash unzstd file_name.bin.zst ``` (This produces `file_name.bin`.) 2. **Move it to your lab share**, e.g.: ```bash mv file_name.bin /n/holylabs/<your_lab>/alphafold_models/ ``` (Create the directory if it doesn't exist.) 3. **Use that lab-share path** as your `--model_dir` argument when running AlphaFold 3. The databases themselves are already available cluster-wide at `/n/holylabs/rc_admin/Everyone/alphafold_databases/v3`, so you don't need to download those. Example SLURM scripts for running AlphaFold 3 (data pipeline on CPU, inference on GPU) are available in the [FASRC User_Codes repo](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/).
✅ Expected Answer
Extract the file Google sends you with `unzstd file.bin.zst`, then place the resulting `file.bin` in a lab share — do NOT put it in netscratch. That location is what you pass as `--model_dir` when you run AlphaFold3.

Question 36: question_36

❓ Question

Before I can run an AlphaFold3 prediction on the cluster, do I need to download the AlphaFold databases into my own lab storage first? If they're already there, what's the path?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-modernization-initiative-faqs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
**No, you do not need to download the AlphaFold databases yourself.** FASRC maintains cluster-wide copies of the databases in a shared location: - **AlphaFold 2 databases:** `/n/holylabs/rc_admin/Everyone/alphafold_databases/v2` - **AlphaFold 3 databases:** `/n/holylabs/rc_admin/Everyone/alphafold_databases/v3` These are stored on SSD storage as recommended by the developers, so you can reference them directly from your jobs without copying them to your own lab storage. **One exception for AlphaFold 3:** You still need to obtain the model parameters from Google yourself. After downloading the `file_name.bin.zst` file from Google, extract it with `unzstd file.bin.zst` and place the resulting `file.bin` in your lab share (not in netscratch). This will be the location you pass as `--model_dir` when running the prediction. For example scripts, see the [FASRC User_Codes repo](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/).
✅ Expected Answer
No download is needed. The AlphaFold database is kept in a cluster-wide location (on SSD, as the developers recommend), so individual users do not have to download it. The AlphaFold3 database is at `/n/holylabs/rc_admin/Everyone/alphafold_databases/v3` (AlphaFold2's is at `/n/holylabs/rc_admin/Everyone/alphafold_databases/v2`). Likewise, the Singularity images are cluster-wide at `/n/singularity_images/FAS/alphafold/`, so you do not have to copy those either.

Question 37: question_37

❓ Question

I copied a friend's sbatch script and stripped it down — it has no partition line, no memory line, and no time line. What does the scheduler actually hand me?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/JobArrays/Exercise_nonsequential_maxarray/array_script.sh, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/submitting-large-numbers-of-jobs, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/IQSS-slurm-examples/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/IQSS-slurm-examples/basicbatch.slurm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mathematica, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
If you omit `#SBATCH` directives for partition, memory, and time, SLURM falls back to its **partition-level defaults**. On FASRC clusters, here’s what you actually get: ### Partition - **Default:** `serial_requeue` > “If you do not specify this parameter you will be given `serial_requeue`” [Running Jobs – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs/) ### Memory - **Default:** 100 MB per job (shared across all cores) > `#SBATCH --mem=100 # Memory shared across all cores in MB` [basicbatch.slurm](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/IQSS-slurm-examples/basicbatch.slurm) ### Time - **Default:** 10 minutes > `#SBATCH -t 15 # Runtime in minutes; job killed after this amount of time; default: 10` [basicbatch.slurm](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/IQSS-slurm-examples/basicbatch.slurm) ### CPUs - **Default:** 1 core > `#SBATCH -n 1 # Number of cores requested; default: 1` [basicbatch.slurm](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/IQSS-slurm-examples/basicbatch.slurm) ### What this means for you Your stripped-down script will run on **1 core, 100 MB RAM, for up to 10 minutes** on the `serial_requeue` partition. If your job needs more resources, it will either fail or be killed prematurely. You’ll want to add explicit directives like: ```bash #SBATCH -p bigmem #SBATCH --mem=32G #SBATCH -t 48:00:00 #SBATCH -n 16 ``` Would you like help tailoring those directives to your workload?
✅ Expected Answer
FASRC's defaults when nothing is requested are: 1 core, 100 MB of memory, 0 GPUs, and the `serial_requeue` partition. Time is the exception — there is no default time limit, so you must always declare a time yourself. The 100 MB memory default is very likely to get your job killed, so you should always specify `--mem` or `--mem-per-cpu`.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Question 38: question_38

❓ Question

I have about 12,000 near-identical runs to do. Can I submit them as one --array job, and does that eat into the number of jobs I'm allowed to have queued?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/job_array.html, slurm.schedmd.com/job_array.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/submitting-large-numbers-of-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/IQSS-slurm-examples/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Example_Recipes/README.md, slurm.schedmd.com/rest_api.html, slurm.schedmd.com/job_array.html, slurm.schedmd.com/openapi_release_notes.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes — you can (and should) use a **single SLURM job array** for 12,000 near-identical runs, but there are two important constraints to be aware of on FASRC: ### 1. Job-array size limit Slurm's `MaxArraySize` parameter caps how many array tasks a single array can contain. The default is **1,001**, and the absolute maximum supported is **4,000,001** [Slurm Job Array Support](https://slurm.schedmd.com/job_array.html). FASRC's default is likely 1,001, so a single `--array=0-11999` submission would exceed it and be rejected. You have two options: - **Split into multiple arrays**: e.g., submit six arrays of 2,000 each (`--array=0-1999`, `--array=2000-3999`, etc.), or smaller chunks that stay under the limit. - **Ask FASRC to temporarily raise `MaxArraySize`** for your account if you truly need one monolithic array (they may or may not allow 12,000 in a single array). ### 2. Does it count toward your job limit? **Yes.** FASRC enforces a **10,000-job limit per user** (scheduled + running combined) [Cluster Customs and Responsibilities](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities/). In Slurm, **each array task counts as an individual job** toward this limit. So 12,000 array tasks would exceed the 10,000-user limit and be rejected with a submit-limit error. ### Recommended approach - **Split into ≤10,000 total tasks** across one or more arrays (e.g., five arrays of 2,000 each = 10,000 total). - **Pause 0.5–1 second between `sbatch` calls** to avoid overwhelming the scheduler [Submitting Large Numbers of Jobs](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/submitting-large-numbers-of-jobs/). - **Request ≥10 minutes** runtime per task to let the scheduler handle setup efficiently [Cluster Customs and Responsibilities](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities/). - **Avoid email notifications** except for errors — too many can get your jobs killed [Submitting Large Numbers of Jobs](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/submitting-large-numbers-of-jobs/). If you need all 12,000 in one batch, consider splitting into multiple submission scripts that run sequentially or using a meta-scheduler to manage them in waves under the 10,000 limit.
✅ Expected Answer
No — you must split it. The maximum array size is 10,000 (that cap applies to both the array index and the array size), so a single 12,000-element array is not allowed. And yes, it counts against your job cap: each array index counts as a single job for the purposes of the Maximum Number of Jobs per User, which is 10,100.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Question 39: question_39

❓ Question

My analysis takes about 3 hours every time on the shared partition, but when I run the exact same thing on serial_requeue it keeps getting killed for hitting the wall clock. What time should I be asking for there?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/kempner-partitions

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
You should ask for **9 hours** on `serial_requeue`. As a rule of thumb, jobs on `serial_requeue` can run on substantially slower hardware than on the shared partition. A good guideline is to allow for a factor of three variance in speed, so if your job takes 3 hours on most hardware, you should request 9 hours on `serial_requeue` [Job Efficiency and Optimization Best Practices](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices/).
✅ Expected Answer
Ask for roughly 9 hours. `serial_requeue` (and `gpu_requeue`) are mosaic partitions made of a wide variety of hardware, so run times vary a lot depending on which host you land on. The rule of thumb is a factor of three variance in speed — so a job that takes 3 hours on most hardware should be given 9 hours on `serial_requeue`. Alternatively, use the `--constraint` option to pin the job to a specific hardware type so the runtime is predictable.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices

Question 40: question_40

❓ Question

My PI bought the nodes in our lab's own partition, and we only ever run there. So why does our fairshare keep dropping — aren't we just using hardware we already paid for?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/kempner-partitions

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
This is a common point of confusion. Even if you only run jobs on your lab’s private partition, **you are still charged against your fairshare**. Here is why: 1. **Fairshare is global:** Fairshare is a cluster-wide metric used to determine job priority across all partitions. To keep the system fair, every user is charged for their usage regardless of which partition they use [Fairshare and Job Accounting](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/). 2. **RawShares vs. Usage:** When you purchase hardware for a private partition, your lab is granted "RawShares" equivalent to that hardware. However, your **fairshare score** is calculated based on your **Share** (your grant) versus your actual **Usage** (what you have consumed). If you are using the hardware you paid for, you are "using up" your share. If you use more than your share over a given period, your fairshare score drops, lowering your job priority. 3. **The "Gratis" Share:** Every lab also receives a base "Gratis Share" that can be used anywhere on the cluster. This is separate from the shares tied to your private hardware. In short, buying hardware increases your **Share** (your entitlement), but it doesn't exempt you from the **Usage** accounting that drives your fairshare score. If your fairshare is dropping, it likely means your lab's actual resource consumption has exceeded the share granted to your account. You can check your current fairshare and usage by running `squeue -u <username>` or checking the fairshare documentation for more details on how TRES (Trackable RESources) are calculated [Fairshare and Job Accounting](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/).
✅ Expected Answer
Because Fairshare is a global quantity on Cannon, not a per-partition one: RawShares are granted to everyone and can be used anywhere on the cluster, so a user is charged regardless of which partition they run in. Groups that own private partitions are granted RawShares equivalent to the hardware in that partition, and that grant exactly offsets the usage of the partition — so it nets out. The upside of this design is flexibility: because the Share is global, a group can leave its own partition idle and spend its shares elsewhere on the cluster instead.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Question 41: question_41

❓ Question

Last week a bunch of our jobs died from node failures and we cancelled several more that were misconfigured. Does that wasted compute still count against our lab's score, and how long until it stops hurting us?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/squeue.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, slurm.schedmd.com/job_state_codes.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/common-pitfalls, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/huce-partitions

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the available documentation, here's what I can tell you about your situation: ### Jobs that died from node failures Jobs that fail due to a `NODE_FAILURE` are automatically requeued by the scheduler [Running Jobs – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs/). This means the compute time used before the failure is considered part of the job's execution and would count against your lab's FairShare score. ### Jobs you cancelled due to misconfiguration If you manually cancelled jobs (using `scancel`), those jobs are marked as `CANCELLED` and the compute time they consumed before cancellation would also count against your FairShare score. ### FairShare score recovery Your FairShare score is calculated based on your lab's actual usage versus their allocated Share [Fairshare and Job Accounting – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/). The score recovers over time as your lab uses less than its allocated Share. You can check your current score by running: ```bash sshare -u <your_username> ``` Your effective score is in the last column, and as a rule of thumb, a score ≤ 0.5 indicates higher priority [FAQ – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq/). To estimate how long it will take to restore your FairShare score, you can use the `scalc` utility [Convenient Slurm Commands – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands/). **Recommendation:** Contact [RCHelp](https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu/) for specific details about your lab's score recovery timeline and to discuss any concerns about the node failures.
✅ Expected Answer
Yes, it still counts. RawUsage is the sum of all usage including failed jobs, jobs that are requeued, and jobs that ran on nodes that failed — FASRC counts it because it is the user's responsibility to use the allocated time effectively even when it is cut short. That usage does decay, though: RawUsage is attenuated by the cluster's half-life, currently 3 days. Work done in the last 3 days counts at full cost, work done 6 days ago costs half, work done 9 days ago one fourth, and so on. To limit the damage from failures, FASRC recommends testing/verifying code before running and enabling checkpointing so jobs can restart where they left off.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Question 42: question_42

❓ Question

I'm a master's student and my advisor wants me to run analyses on the Nielsen consumer panel data. Can I be added to the agreement?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/nielsen-dataset

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/nielsen-dataset, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/openai-guidelines, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-use-agreements, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/other-institutions

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the Nielsen Dataset agreement, **no, you cannot be added to the agreement if you are a master's student**. The agreement explicitly states: > "Data and License is made solely to the individual researcher via Nielsen site. This research can be Ph.D. student or postdoc that has a tenure-track faculty registered on the site. **Data is not available to undergraduates or masters students.**" [Nielsen Dataset – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/nielsen-dataset/) Additionally, the Data Access and Confidentiality Agreement notes that data may only be disclosed to "PhD seeking students of Harvard working under the supervision or direction of Researcher on the Research Project(s)." Your advisor (a tenure-track faculty member) would need to be the registered researcher on the Nielsen site, and only PhD students working under their supervision would be eligible for data access.
✅ Expected Answer
No. Under the Master Access Agreement, the Nielsen data and license are made solely to the individual researcher registered via the Chicago Booth Nielsen site. That researcher can be a Ph.D. student or a postdoc who has a tenure-track faculty member registered on the site — but the data is explicitly not available to undergraduates or master's students. Relatedly, under the Data Access and Confidentiality Agreement the Researcher may disclose the Data only to PhD-seeking students of Harvard working under their supervision on the Research Project.

Question 44: question_44

❓ Question

I want to spin up a few hundred cores on Cannon to crawl a public website faster. It's for academic research — any issue with that?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/web-scraping-policy

Retrieved Documents: github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/GPU_Computing/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/common-pitfalls, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/acceptable-use, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/web-scraping-policy, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasrc-ai-facilitation, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/openai-guidelines, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasrc-cluster-storage-policy

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes — there are significant issues with using the Cannon cluster for web crawling/scraping, even for academic research. **FASRC's official policy is to discourage using the cluster for scraping** ([Web Scraping Policy](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/web-scraping-policy/)). The key concerns are: 1. **Cluster is easily weaponized** — Because Cannon has hundreds of cores and parallelization is easy, scraping from the cluster can generate massive, concentrated traffic that could negatively impact target websites and have consequences for other researchers. 2. **You represent Harvard** — Your actions on the cluster are attributable to Harvard University, which can lead to IP blacklisting or even legal action against the university. 3. **Academic status is not an exemption** — Being involved in academic pursuits does not exempt you from the usage policies of websites or social media platforms. **What FASRC recommends instead:** - **Scrape elsewhere first** — Do the data acquisition on your own machine or a dedicated scraping service, then bring the data to the cluster for processing. - **Contact FASRC before starting** — If your research truly requires scraping from the cluster, you must contact FASRC before commencing any scraping activity. - **Check site policies** — Respect robots.txt files, Terms of Service, and any stated policies about automated access or AI/ML data collection. **Bottom line:** Don't spin up hundreds of cores on Cannon to crawl a website. Do the scraping on your own hardware or a purpose-built service, then transfer the data to Cannon for analysis. If you have questions, reach out to FASRC support first.
✅ Expected Answer
Yes, several. FASRC highly discourages using the cluster itself to scrape: because of its size and ease of parallelization, the cluster is easily weaponized and your actions could have consequences for other researchers, so you should seek another avenue for data acquisition first. You must contact FASRC before commencing any scraping activity using the FASRC cluster. It is highly preferable that you do the scraping elsewhere and then bring the data to the cluster for processing — and if the data is sensitive, confidential, contains human data, or it is unclear, doing it elsewhere is a requirement. Highly parallelized scraping is specifically called out as risking blacklisting of the cluster's or the university's IP range, which would harm other users. Being an academic does not exempt you from a site's usage policies.

Question 45: question_45

❓ Question

I logged in to Globus and picked the Harvard FAS RC Holyoke collection, but I can't browse into my home directory and the Everyone folder in my lab share doesn't show up either. Is my account misconfigured?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/academic-use

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Your account is **not misconfigured** — this is expected behavior. Here's what's happening: ### Home directories are not available via Globus Home directories cannot be shared through Globus under any circumstances, as this would create a security vulnerability (authentication keys/passwords may be shared inadvertently) [Globus File Transfer – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/). ### The "Everyone" folder is not available via Globus Globus can only see folders inside `Lab` and `Users/$USER`. The `Everyone` folder (and `Transfer`) is **not available through Globus** — it is intended only for internal sharing on the HPC cluster [Globus File Transfer – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/). ### What you *can* access via Globus Globus can only see folders inside `Lab` and `Users/$USER`. Any folder must be inside one of these two locations. Your lab share should be visible under the `Lab` directory in the Harvard FAS RC Holyoke collection. If your lab share itself doesn't appear in the Holyoke collection, that would be a different issue — in that case, please contact FASRC support. But the absence of your home directory and the Everyone folder is by design.
✅ Expected Answer
Nothing is misconfigured — both are expected. Home directories are not and will not be added to Globus under any circumstances, because sharing home directories creates a security vulnerability (authentication keys/passwords could be shared inadvertently). And within a lab share, Globus can only see the `Lab` and `Users/$USER` folders — anything outside those, such as `Everyone` and `Transfer`, is not available through Globus (note the `Users` folder has been deprecated as of 2025). Also be aware symlinks do not work in Globus. If your lab share genuinely lacks a `Lab` directory or is not visible at all in the Holyoke/Boston collections, contact FASRC.
